=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
On a circle, there are N equally spaced points numbered 0,1,\ldots,N-1 in this order, with Alice at point 0 and Bob at point K. Initially, all points are colored white. Starting with Alice, they alternately perform the following operation:

- Choose one of the currently white points and color it black. Here, after the operation, the coloring of the points must be symmetric with respect to the straight line connecting the operator and the center of the circle.

If the operator cannot perform an operation satisfying the above condition, the sequence of operations ends there.
Both players cooperate and make the best choices to maximize the total number of points colored black in the end. Determine whether all points are colored black at the end of the sequence of operations.
You are given T test cases to solve.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
T
\mathrm{case}_1
\mathrm{case}_2
\vdots 
\mathrm{case}_T

Each test case \mathrm{case}_i (1 \leq i \leq T) is in the following format:
N K

Output

Print T lines. The i-th line should contain Yes if all points can be colored black for the i-th test case, and No otherwise.

Constraints


- 1 \leq T \leq 10^5
- 2 \leq N \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq K \leq N-1
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

4
6 2
6 3
6 1
200000 100000

Sample Output 1

Yes
No
Yes
No

For N=6 and K=2, all points can be colored black by, for example, performing operations in the following order:

- Alice colors point 3 black.
- Bob colors point 1 black.
- Alice colors point 5 black.
- Bob colors point 2 black.
- Alice colors point 4 black.
- Bob colors point 0 black.


For N=6 and K=3, below is one possible progression. Actually, no matter what they do, they cannot color all points black.

- Alice colors point 3 black.
- Bob colors point 0 black.
- Alice cannot color any point black so that the coloring will be symmetric with respect to her line, so she cannot perform the operation.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Thus the game decomposes into "chains" within components: Starting from a synchronized state (component invariant under both), a free move by current player colors a fixed point of their reflection, creating a component that is invariant under current but not other. This is a combinatorial scheduling problem over components. But maybe the startability of a component is tied to its size parity and type, making it always startable by both except some special cases. Let's investigate.

First, define full group orbit components and their structure.

We have N points, reflections r_A: i -> -i, r_B: i -> 2K - i. The group generated has order 2M where M = N/g, g=gcd(N,2K). Full group orbits:
- Self-negative residue classes c ≡ -c mod g: c=0, and if g even c=g/2. These are cycles under ρ of length M, and r_A/r_B act as reflections on a regular M-gon (type S). Number s = 1 if g odd, 2 if g even.
- Other pairs of residue classes {c, -c}: type P, size 2M. Number p = (g - s)/2.

Let's analyze startability for type S (regular M-gon with adjacent reflections).

But wait, what if g>2 but typeP components might be startable if the starting reflection has fixed points in them? We said typeP has no fixed points for either reflection because r_A and r_B map residue class c to -c, and c≠-c. But is it possible a point in C_c is fixed by r_A even if c≠-c? Fixed point i satisfies i ≡ -i mod N, so i ≡ -i mod g, so c=-c. Thus no. Similarly r_B: i ≡ 2K - i mod N; mod g, 2K≡0, so i≡ -i mod g. Thus no. So typeP cannot be started by any player. Now, need revisit the assumption that every component must be colored by a single chain starting from empty and ending full, and that no component can be partially synchronized. We argued synchronized subset invariant under both reflections is union of full group orbits. Within a full group orbit (component), graph connected, so only empty/full. Need prove graph connected for type S and type P. The full group orbit by definition is connected under generators r_A,r_B (as graph). Yes.

But is it possible to have a legal move that colors a point in a component that is not synchronized but not at a chain boundary? The global state after each move is invariant under current reflection. At a chain boundary, all components are invariant under both reflections (synchronized). Is it guaranteed that after a component becomes full, all components are synchronized? If a chain ends when the component becomes full, yes. But could a chain end earlier with a synchronized partial subset? No because only empty/full. Could a chain "end" by the game stopping before all components full? That would be max not all. For all black, each component must eventually full; at the moment a component becomes full, it is synchronized. The other components at that moment are invariant under the current player's reflection (because after the move that filled it, global set is current-invariant). Are they also invariant under the other reflection? Wait at a chain boundary, the next player is about to move. For the next player to be free or forced? Let's define chain boundary as a state from which the next move is a free move (starting a new component). A free move requires before the move all components are invariant under the current player's reflection. Since after previous move, all components are invariant under the previous player's reflection. For the current player to be free, all components must also be invariant under current reflection, i.e., synchronized. Thus at a free move, all unfilled components are empty (synchronized) and filled components full. Therefore yes, chain boundaries are global synchronized states. Is every transition from one synchronized state to the next necessarily colors an entire component? Starting from synchronized (all components empty/full), current player chooses an empty component and colors a fixed point. Then the other player is forced to move in that same component until it becomes full, because until then the component is not synchronized and is the only unsynchronized component; the other player cannot start a new component because that would leave this component unsynchronized. Let's verify: After Alice free move in component X, X is A-invariant but not B-invariant (unless X size1? If X size1, after coloring the only point, X is full and synchronized; chain length1). If X not full, Bob must move in X to make it B-invariant. After Bob moves, X may become full (synchronized) or B-invariant but not A-invariant. If not full, Alice forced in X, etc. So indeed a chain colors one component fully. If the free move colors the last point of a component? But empty component, size maybe1; then full. Good.

Thus global process is concatenation of component chains.

Now, startability of a type S component by a player P: We said iff P's reflection has at least one fixed point in that component. Need prove if it has a fixed point, the chain of length M works. We gave explicit sequence in standard coordinates. But need ensure for any type S component, the two reflections are adjacent (product rotation by one step) and the starting reflection with a fixed point can be put in standard form R0: x->-x, R1: x->1-x (or maybe R1: x->1-x). Let's verify for all type S components, including when g=2 and M odd/even. The product r_B r_A = ρ is rotation by 2K. On a self component C_c, ρ acts as a cycle of length M. The two reflections r_A and r_B restricted to C_c satisfy r_B r_A = ρ (one step along the cycle). Thus in a suitable labeling, r_A: x -> a - x, r_B: x -> a+1 - x (or a? Let's derive). If r_A has a fixed point, we can choose coordinate origin at that fixed point, so r_A: x -> -x. Then r_B = ρ r_A: x -> ρ(-x). Since ρ is +1 in the cycle coordinate, r_B(x) = -x + 1 = 1 - x. Yes. So standard. Starting with r_A, sequence works. If starting with r_B and r_B has a fixed point, we can similarly set r_B: x -> -x, r_A: x -> 1 - x? Since r_A r_B = ρ^{-1} maybe. The sequence works symmetrically. So startability condition holds.

Now, for g=2 case, let's re-evaluate startability and scheduling more formally.

g=2, N=2M, K satisfies gcd(N,2K)=2 => gcd(M,K)=1? Since N=2M, gcd(2M,2K)=2*gcd(M,K). For this to be2, gcd(M,K)=1. Yes.

Components:
C0 = {0,2,4,...,2M-2} (even)
C1 = {1,3,5,...,2M-1} (odd)
Each size M.

r_A fixed points: 0 and M (since N/2=M). If M odd, M is odd -> one in each. If M even, M even -> both in C0.
r_B fixed points: K and K+M (since N even). Since gcd(M,K)=1:
- If M odd, K and K+M have opposite parity -> one in each.
- If M even, K must be odd (gcd(even M,K)=1), so K and K+M are both odd -> both in C1.
Thus:
M odd: both components have at least one fixed point for both reflections. Startable by both.
M even: C0 has r_A fixed only, C1 has r_B fixed only. Startable by Alice only for C0, Bob only for C1.

Let's decide final code:
import sys, math
def solve():
    data=sys.stdin.read().split()
    if not data: return
    t=int(data[0])
    out=[]
    idx=1
    for _ in range(t):
        N=int(data[idx]); K=int(data[idx+1]); idx+=2
        if N % 2 == 1:
            ok = math.gcd(N, K) == 1
        elif N % 4 == 2:
            ok = math.gcd(N//2, K) == 1
        else:
            ok = False
        out.append("Yes" if ok else "No")
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))
if __name__=="__main__": solve()

Move 1 (R0): color 0.
For t=1,2,...:
Move 2t (R1): color t.
Move 2t+1 (R0): color -t.
After move 2t: black set {- (t-1), ..., t} (size 2t). After move 2t+1: black set {-t, ..., t} (size 2t+1). This is legal by induction. It stops when all residues mod M are covered, after M moves. But before final, let's revisit the problem statement: "On a circle, there are N equally spaced points numbered 0,1,...,N-1 in this order, with Alice at point 0 and Bob at point K. Initially, all points are colored white. Starting with Alice, they alternately perform the following operation:
- Choose one of the currently white points and color it black. Here, after the operation, the coloring of the points must be symmetric with respect to the straight line connecting the operator and the center of the circle.
If the operator cannot perform an operation satisfying the above condition, the sequence of operations ends there.
Both players cooperate and make the best choices to maximize the total number of points colored black in the end. Determine whether all points are colored black at the end of the sequence of operations."

Let's maybe write final code with function:
import sys
from math import gcd

def main():
    it = iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())
    t = int(next(it))
    ans = []
    for _ in range(t):
        N = int(next(it)); K = int(next(it))
        if N & 1:
            ok = gcd(N, K) == 1
        elif N % 4 == 2:
            ok = gcd(N // 2, K) == 1
        else:
            ok = False
        ans.append("Yes" if ok else "No")
    print("\n".join(ans))
if __name__ == "__main__": main()

Let's double-check the code syntax:
import sys
from math import gcd

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
    if not data:
        return
    t = int(data[0])
    out = []
    idx = 1
    for _ in range(t):
        N = int(data[idx]); K = int(data[idx+1]); idx += 2
        if N & 1:
            ok = gcd(N, K) == 1
        elif N % 4 == 2:
            ok = gcd(N // 2, K) == 1
        else:
            ok = False
        out.append("Yes" if ok else "No")
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
Good.

Now, let's maybe think if there is any issue with "N=124,K=3" if "No" Good.

Now, let's maybe think if there is any issue with "N=124,K=4" if "No" Good.

Now, let's maybe think if there is any issue with "N=
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys
from math import gcd

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
    if not data:
        return

    t = int(data[0])
    ans = []
    idx = 1

    for _ in range(t):
        N = int(data[idx])
        K = int(data[idx + 1])
        idx += 2

        if N & 1:
            ok = gcd(N, K) == 1
        elif N % 4 == 2:
            ok = gcd(N // 2, K) == 1
        else:
            ok = False

        ans.append("Yes" if ok else "No")

    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(ans))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
